REASONING_CONTENT
No event type handling in 8.2.8. So the feature is indeed new.

I'll go with a pragmatic parser:
- Split the sequence body by `;` after removing the trailing `:event_type`.
- To handle event_type reliably, I'll use a regex where the text group does NOT greedily consume a trailing `:single-digit` when that single-digit is
followed by `u`. Specifically, use a lookahead or negative approach.

Let me write a regex that captures:
```
\A
\x1b\[
(?P<number>\d+)
(?:;(?P<modifiers>\d+))?
(?:;(?P<text>(?:\d+:)*\d+))?
(?::(?P<event_type>\d+))?
(?P<end>[u~ABCDEFHPQRS])
\Z
```
The issue remains text greediness. But we can make the regex use `text` non-greedy AND event_type single digit:
```
(?P<text>(?:\d+:)*?\d+?)?
(?::(?P<event_type>[1-9]))?
```
For `97;1;65:2u`: text non-greedy tries `6`, then `65`, then event_type tries `2` -> match. Good.
For `97;1;65:97u`: text tries `65`, event_type tries `97` — `[1-9]` only matches `9` but then needs `u` after; `9` is followed by `7`, fails. Then
text backtracks to `65:97`, event_type empty -> match text as two codepoints. Good.
For `97;1;65:12u`: event_type `[1-9]` can't match `12`, so text backtracks and matches `65:12` as two codepoints — misparse if 12 was event_type, but
12 isn't a valid event type anyway.
For `97;1;65:1u`: text non-greedy matches `65`, event_type matches `1`. Good.

But what about text that is a single digit codepoint, e.g., text=`2` and no event_type: `97;1;2u`. Regex: text non-greedy tries `2`, then event_type
`[1-9]` would try to match `2` but then needs `u` — after `2` is `u`, so event_type matches `2`, text would be... let's see. With text non-greedy, it
might try text=`2` and then event_type has nothing to match (since `u` follows), so it matches text=`2`, event_type=None. Good. But with text=`2` and
event_type=`2`: `97;1;2:2u`. Text non-greedy tries `2`, then event_type tries `2` -> matches. Good.

However, there's a subtle regex backtracking issue with non-greedy text and multi-digit text. Let me just test it. But first, is `[1-9]` the right
restriction for event_type? The spec's event types are 1-9 (single digit). Let me check: Kitty protocol docs say event_type is an integer from 1-9?

- 3: release

- 6: ...
- 7: ...
- 8: ...
- 9: ...

Wait, but what about `\x1b[97;3:2u` (no text)? Regex:
- number=97, modifiers=3, text group? The text group requires `;` before it: `(?:;(?P<text>...))?`. Here after `97;3` we have `:2u`, not `;`, so text
group is None. Then event_type group `(?::(?P<event_type>[1-9]))?` matches `:2`. Good.

Now, here's the critical part about "base_key", "shifted_key", "base_layout_key".

The Kitty protocol:
- `number` is the key code. In "disambiguate escape codes" mode (the most common for Kitty protocol), `number` is the *base* key code (the unshifted
key). E.g., Shift+a -> `CSI 97;2u` (code 97 = 'a', shift modifier). Shift+`=` -> `CSI 61;2u` (code 61 = '=', shift modifier), and the shifted
character is `+`.
- In "report all keys as escape codes" mode, `number` may be the *alternate* (shifted) key code. E.g., Shift+a -> `CSI 65;2u` (code 65 = 'A', shift
modifier). But even then, the shift modifier is present.
- With text reporting, `CSI 97;2;65u` gives base code 97, shift modifier, text "A".

To compute `base_key` and `shifted_key`:
- `base_key`: name of the unshifted key from `number`. For `number=97` -> "a". For `number=65` (alternate reporting), the base key is actually 'a'
(because shift is in modifiers), so we should derive base_key by unshifting: if shift modifier is present and `number` is an uppercase letter, convert
to lowercase. Similarly for symbols? Harder.
- `shifted_key`: name of the shifted character. From `text` if present; otherwise if shift modifier is present and `number` is a letter, shift it
(uppercase); otherwise maybe same as base_key.

### Algorithm for parsing a Kitty key event

Inputs: `number`, `modifiers` (bitmask), `text` (list of characters or None), `event_type`, `end`.

Actually, let me verify: Kitty keyboard protocol progressive enhancement modes:
  - `CSI > 1 u`: disambiguate escape codes (modifiers always reported; alternate keys? "the key codes for modifiers are reported as the base key").
  - `CSI > 2 u`: report alternate keys (when shift is pressed, the key code is the shifted character's code, e.g., 'A').
  - `CSI > 3 u`: report all keys as escape codes (also includes text?).

Hmm, so whether `number=65` with shift means "base key A" or "base key a with alternate A" depends on mode. Textual likely requests `CSI > 1 u`

already treats an uppercase code with shift as the base lowercase key. Good.

### Determining base key and shifted key

Let me define a US keyboard shift mapping for symbols, since Kitty protocol with disambiguate mode reports base codes, and we need shifted_key names.
The task explicitly mentions `shifted_key="plus"` for the `=` key (Shift+= produces +). So we need a mapping:
- `=` (equals_sign) -> `+` (plus)
- `1` -> `!` (exclamation_mark)
- `2` -> `@` (at)
- `3` -> `#` (number_sign)
- `4` -> `$` (dollar_sign)
- `5` -> `%` (percent_sign)
- `6` -> `^` (circumflex_accent)
- `7` -> `&` (ampersand)
- `8` -> `*` (asterisk)
- `9` -> `(` (left_parenthesis)
- `0` -> `)` (right_parenthesis)
- `-` -> `_` (underscore)
- `=` -> `+` (plus)
- `[` -> `{` (left_curly_bracket)
- `]` -> `}` (right_curly_bracket)
- `\` -> `|` (vertical_line)
- `;` -> `:` (colon)
- `'` -> `"` (quotation_mark)
- `,` -> `<` (less_than_sign)
- `.` -> `>` (greater_than_sign)
- `/` -> `?` (question_mark)
- `` ` `` -> `~` (tilde)

And for letters: lowercase -> uppercase.

Now, "base_layout_key": what is this? In the Kitty protocol, there's a concept where the reported key code is the "key code" which is
layout-independent (the physical key), and "text" is the character produced in the current layout. `base_layout_key` would be the key name in the base
layout. Since Textual doesn't know the layout, we can default `base_layout_key = base_key`. The task says "exact stored fields phase, modifiers,
base_key, shifted_key, and base_layout_key" — it just requires the field to exist and be populated reasonably.

Let me now define the parse algorithm more concretely.

For a single Kitty event (one text codepoint or no text):

```
number = int(number_str)
modifier_bits = int(modifiers_str) - 1 if modifiers_str else 0
event_type = int(event_type_str) if event_type_str else 1
modifiers = sorted(name for bit, name in MODIFIER_BITS.items() if modifier_bits & bit)

# Functional key?
functional = FUNCTIONAL_KEYS.get(f"{number}{end}", "")
if number == 0:
    # associated text only: use text as both key and character
    if text:
        key = _character_to_key(text)  # if text is single char
        character = text
    else:
        key = "unknown"?
elif functional:
    key = functional
    ...
else:
    base_char = chr(number)
    if "shift" in modifiers and base_char.isupper():
        base_char = base_char.lower()
    base_key = _character_to_key(base_char)
    ...
```

Wait, but if `number == 0` and text is present, what about modifiers? E.g., `CSI 0;3;65u` (alt+text "A")? The task says "associated-text-only key-code
0 uses its text as both key and character". It doesn't specify modifiers handling. If code 0, there's no base key; the text IS the key. Should we
still include modifiers in the public key? Probably yes: key = "alt+A"? But then "text as both key and character" — hmm. Let me keep it simple: for
code 0, key = text (with modifiers if any), character = text.

But wait — if code 0 with text "A" and alt modifier, the "text as both key and character" might mean key="A", character="A", ignoring alt? The task
says "associated-text-only key-code 0 uses its text as both key and character" — likely means for `CSI 0;1;65u` -> key="A", character="A". I'll
include modifiers in the public key if present, but the base key metadata would be the text.

Let me look at the actual Kitty protocol docs for code 0:
> If the key code is 0, it means the event is for a key that has associated text but no key code, and the text field contains the text.

So for `CSI 0;1;65u`, key should be "A", character "A".

Now, the algorithm for public key construction:

For Kitty events (end == "u"), the current behavior constructs:
`key = "+".join(sorted_modifiers + [key])`.

We need to decide:
- Which key name to use in the public key: base_key, shifted_key, or text?
- When to use uppercase vs lowercase.

From the task:
- shift-only printable: public key may be either "A" or "shift+a". The existing tests in 7.5.0 expect `\x1b[65;4u` -> "alt+shift+a" (lowercase). So to
preserve existing behavior, for modified keys, use lowercase base_key. For shift-only, we can choose either. Let me check what the 8.2.8 produces:
`\x1b[65;4u` -> "alt+shift+A" (uppercase). That's a change from 7.5.0 which had "alt+shift+a". The task says "Preserve printable semantics" and
"Non-shift modified printable shortcuts must keep names like 'alt+shift+a' with character=None" — note "alt+shift+a" lowercase. So we should keep
lowercase base_key in public key for modified shortcuts.

- shift-only `CSI 65;2u`: public key may be "A" or "shift+a". Which one should we produce? Let me think about what's least disruptive. In 7.5.0,
`\x1b[65;2u` (not in tests but likely) would produce "shift+a" (since key_tokens append key.lower()). So to preserve existing behavior, "shift+a" with
character="A" seems right. But wait, "the public key may be either 'A' or 'shift+a'" — the test will accept either. I'll keep "shift+a" (lowercase)
for consistency with existing behavior, since existing 7.5.0 produced "shift+a".

Actually, let me check 7.5.0 behavior for `\x1b[65;2u`:

So 7.5.0:
- `CSI 97;2u` -> key="shift+a", character=None (task wants character="A")
- `CSI 65;2u` -> key="shift+a" (task wants character="A", base_key="a")
- `CSI 61;2u` -> key="shift+equals_sign", character=None (task wants shifted_key="plus")
- `CSI 97u` -> key="a", character="a"

So the minimal change for shift-only: keep key="shift+a" but set character="A" and populate metadata.

Now let me think about aliases. The task: "alternate metadata uses Textual names like shifted_key='plus' and alias ctrl+plus."

For `CSI 61;5u` (ctrl+shift+equals), the public key should probably be "ctrl+shift+equals_sign" or "ctrl+plus"? The task says "alias ctrl+plus" —
meaning the Key event should have "ctrl+plus" in its aliases so bindings to `ctrl+plus` match. Currently `Key.aliases = _get_key_aliases(key)` where
`_get_key_aliases` returns `[key] + KEY_ALIASES.get(key, [])`. So if key is "ctrl+shift+equals_sign", aliases won't include "ctrl+plus". We need to
add alternate key aliases.

How should aliases work? For a key with shifted_key="plus" and base_key="equals_sign", with modifiers ctrl+shift:
- public key: "ctrl+shift+equals_sign" (or "ctrl+plus"?)
- aliases should include "ctrl+plus" (the shifted form without shift modifier, since shift is implicit in the alternate key).

Actually, in Textual, `ctrl+plus` is a binding name for Ctrl+Shift+= (since Ctrl+plus requires shift on US layout to type +). So the alias should map
the shifted key name "plus" with non-shift modifiers to the full name "ctrl+shift+equals_sign". More generally:
- For a key where shift is held and the shifted_key differs from base_key, we want an alias that omits "shift" but uses shifted_key: e.g.,
key="ctrl+shift+equals_sign" gets alias "ctrl+plus".
- Also maybe key="shift+equals_sign" gets alias "plus"? But `plus` alone would conflict with the actual plus key. Hmm, but `plus` is itself a key
(Shift+= on US layout). If the terminal reports `CSI 43;2u` (code 43='+', shift), base_key should be "equals_sign", shifted_key "plus". Public key
"shift+equals_sign". Should alias include "plus"? Probably yes so that binding "plus" works? But the task specifically says "alias ctrl+plus" for the
ctrl case. Let me focus on that.

Let me think about the alias generation more generally. We have:
- `modifiers`: e.g., ("ctrl", "shift")
- `base_key`: "equals_sign"
- `shifted_key`: "plus"

The public key could be:
- `ctrl+shift+equals_sign` (using base_key)
- or `ctrl+plus` (using shifted_key, dropping shift)

The task says "alternate metadata uses Textual names like shifted_key='plus' and alias ctrl+plus". This suggests the public key might be
`ctrl+shift+equals_sign`, and aliases include `ctrl+plus`. Or public key = `ctrl+plus` and alias = `ctrl+shift+equals_sign`. Either way, both should
match.

  3860	    def action_help_quit(self) -> None:
  3861	        """Bound to ctrl+C to alert the user that it no longer quits."""
  3862	        # Doing this because users will reflexively hit ctrl+C to exit
  3863	        # Ctrl+C is now bound to copy if an input / textarea is focused.

    42	        """Raise exception for case where user presses a key and there are multiple candidate key handler methods for it."""

    45	            f"We found both {first_handler!r} and {second_handler!r}, "

So aliases are used in `_dispatch_key` for key handler methods (`key_<name>`). For binding matching (`_check_bindings`), only `event.key` is used. So
to make `ctrl+plus` binding match, the `event.key` must literally be `ctrl+plus`, OR the binding matching would need to check aliases (it doesn't).
Therefore, for `CSI 61;5u` (ctrl+shift+equals), the public `event.key` should probably be `ctrl+plus` so that a binding `ctrl+plus` matches. The task
says "alias ctrl+plus" — maybe the public key is `ctrl+shift+equals_sign` and the alias `ctrl+plus` is used for `key_` handler dispatch (since
`name_aliases` includes it). But bindings wouldn't match. Hmm.

Wait, maybe the intended public key for `CSI 61;5u` is `ctrl+plus` and the alias is `ctrl+shift+equals_sign`. Let me re-read: "alternate metadata uses
Textual names like shifted_key='plus' and alias ctrl+plus." This phrase is ambiguous — "alias ctrl+plus" could mean "the alias [is] ctrl+plus" or
"alias [includes] ctrl+plus".

Let me think about what makes sense for Textual. In Textual, bindings are typically declared as `"ctrl+plus"` for Ctrl+Shift+=. The terminal sends
`CSI 61;5u` (code 61 = '=' base, modifiers ctrl+shift). The current parser produces key="ctrl+shift+equals_sign". That won't match a `ctrl+plus`
binding. The fix "alternate-key shortcuts stop matching shifted forms" means exactly this: shortcuts declared with the shifted form (`ctrl+plus`) stop
matching when the terminal reports the base key with shift modifier (`ctrl+shift+equals_sign`). So the fix should make the public key (or at least
binding matching) use the shifted form.

Given `_check_bindings` only uses `event.key`, the public key should be `ctrl+plus` for that event. So for `CSI 61;5u`, key should be `ctrl+plus`
(using shifted_key "plus", dropping shift from modifiers because shift is implied by the shifted key). And aliases would include
`ctrl+shift+equals_sign` for handler dispatch.

But wait, then what about the earlier requirement: "Non-shift modified printable shortcuts must keep names like 'alt+shift+a' with character=None" —
for `alt+shift+a`, the shifted key is "A", and public key stays "alt+shift+a" (using base_key "a", not shifted "A"). Why would `alt+shift+a` use
base_key but `ctrl+plus` use shifted_key? Because "alt+shift+a" is a printable letter where the shifted form is the same letter uppercase; Textual
convention is lowercase base key. But for `ctrl+plus`, the base key is "equals_sign" and the shifted key is "plus"; Textual convention uses the *name
of the character produced* ("plus") because that's how users think of the shortcut.

Hmm, but why not use "alt+shift+plus" for Shift+Alt+=? The task says "alternate-key shortcuts stop matching shifted forms" — the alternate key is the
shifted form. When shift is held and the shifted key has a distinct name (like "plus" vs "equals_sign"), Textual should use the shifted name in the
public key. When the shifted form is just an uppercase letter, the base lowercase name is used (existing convention).

Let me reconsider the general rule:
- Compute `shifted_key` (name of the character produced by shift).
- If `shifted_key == base_key` (no distinct shifted form, e.g., letters map a->A but names are "a" vs "A"), use base_key.
- Actually for letters, `_character_to_key("a")` = "a", `_character_to_key("A")` = "A" (since isalnum, no Unicode name). The public key convention
uses lowercase "a", not "A". But `shifted_key` metadata should be "A" maybe? The task says "base_key stays 'a'" and "modifiers reports ('shift',)". It
doesn't say shifted_key for the 'A' case. It says "shifted_key='plus'" for the equals case. So `shifted_key` is the *name of the shifted character*:
for Shift+a, shifted_key would be "A" (uppercase), and base_key "a". But the public key uses base_key "shift+a".

For `=` with shift: shifted_key = "plus", base_key = "equals_sign". Public key for ctrl+shift: "ctrl+plus" (shifted form). For alt+shift: maybe
"alt+plus"? Hmm but the task example "alt+shift+a" uses base form.

I think the rule for public key is:
- If `shifted_key` is a "letter" (one-char and differs only by case from base), use base_key.
- Otherwise (shifted_key is a distinct name like "plus"), use shifted_key.

Actually, let me look at what makes sense with `key_to_character`. `key_to_character("ctrl+plus")` returns None (because of modifier), so character is
None. Fine.

Let me look at the existing `Keys` enum for "plus": there's no `plus` entry, but `KEY_NAME_REPLACEMENTS` maps "plus_sign" -> "plus". And
`format_key("plus")` -> "+" (via unicode lookup). So `ctrl+plus` is a valid binding name.

### Detailed algorithm for `_parse_extended_key`

Let me define constants:

```python
MODIFIER_BITS: Final = (
    ("shift", 1),
    ("alt", 2),
    ("ctrl", 4),
    ("super", 8),
    ("hyper", 16),
    ("meta", 32),
)
```

Actually order for sorting should be alphabetical: "alt", "ctrl", "hyper", "meta", "shift", "super". The task example says `modifiers reports
('shift',)` for shift-only and `('alt', 'ctrl')` for alt+ctrl. So sorted alphabetically.

`_PHASE_FROM_EVENT_TYPE = {1: "press", 2: "repeat", 3: "release"}`

US shift mapping:
```python
_SHIFTED_CHARACTERS: Final = {
    "1": "!", "2": "@", "3": "#", "4": "$", "5": "%",
    "6": "^", "7": "&", "8": "*", "9": "(", "0": ")",
    "-": "_", "=": "+", "[": "{", "]": "}", "\\": "|",
    ";": ":", "'": '"', ",": "<", ".": ">", "/": "?",
    "`": "~",
}
```

Now the function:

```python
@lru_cache(maxsize=1024)
def _parse_extended_key(self, sequence: str) -> list[events.Key] | None:
    if (match := _re_extended_key.fullmatch(sequence)) is None:
        return None

    number_str = match["number"]
    modifiers_str = match["modifiers"]
    text_str = match["text"]
    event_type_str = match["event_type"]
    end = match["end"]

    codepoint = int(number_str or "1")
    modifier_bits = int(modifiers_str) - 1 if modifiers_str else 0
    event_type = int(event_type_str) if event_type_str else 1
    phase = _PHASE_FROM_EVENT_TYPE.get(event_type, "press")
    modifiers = _modifiers_from_bits(modifier_bits)  # sorted tuple

    # functional key?
    functional_key = FUNCTIONAL_KEYS.get(f"{codepoint}{end}", "")

    text_characters = self._parse_colon_codepoints(text_str)

    key_events = []
    for text in text_characters:
        ... build one Key event ...
    return key_events
```

Wait, the text field can contain multiple codepoints (composed), which yields multiple Key events? In 8.2.8, yes, it iterates over each codepoint and
yields a separate Key event. For a composed character (e.g., "e" + combining accent), they'd be separate keys. That's fine.

Now for each text value (str or None), build the event.

Let me define a helper to compute the key names:

```python
def _kitty_key_names(codepoint: int, text: str | None, modifiers: tuple[str, ...], end: str) -> tuple[str, str, str]:
    """Return (base_key, shifted_key, public_key_base)."""
```

Let me work through cases.

**Case A: functional key** (e.g., enter, backspace, home, f1):
- `functional_key = FUNCTIONAL_KEYS.get(f"{codepoint}{end}", "")`
- base_key = shifted_key = functional_key
- public key base = functional_key
- character: if text provided, use text; else special char (backspace -> "\x7f", enter -> "\r", tab -> "\t") or None? For functional keys, character
is typically None (current 7.5.0 gives character=None for `\x1b[13~`? Let me check: earlier `\x1b[13~` gave char=None, and `\x1b[127u` gave
char=None). The 8.2.8 uses `SPECIAL_KEY_TO_CHARACTER` to give backspace="\x7f", enter="\r", tab="\t" when no modifiers. The task doesn't require
changing this, but "legacy ... Backspace" suggests maybe character for backspace should stay as whatever. Let me keep 8.2.8 behavior: special char
when no modifiers.
- modifiers in public key: all modifiers, sorted, then base key.
- For a functional key, shift modifier doesn't change the key name (shift+enter, shift+backspace). So public key = "shift+enter" etc. base_key =
"enter", shifted_key = "enter".

**Case B: code 0 (associated text only)**:
- text must be present. key = text (as the key name), character = text.
- base_key = shifted_key = base_layout_key = key (or maybe text).
- modifiers: include in public key? Let me check "uses its text as both key and character". For code 0 with no modifiers, key = text. With modifiers,
probably key = "alt+" + text. I'll include modifiers.
- But `_character_to_key(text)` for text="A" returns "A" (uppercase), so key="A". For text="a" returns "a". For text="+" returns "plus". Good.

**Case C: normal printable/non-functional key**:
- base_character = chr(codepoint)
- if "shift" in modifiers and base_character.isupper(): base_character = base_character.lower()  # alternate key reporting
- base_key = _character_to_key(base_character)
- base_layout_key = base_key

  - if text is not None: shifted_text = text
  - else:
    - if "shift" in modifiers:
      - if base_character.isalpha(): shifted_char = base_character.upper()
      - elif base_character in SHIFT_MAP: shifted_char = SHIFT_MAP[base_character]
      - else: shifted_char = base_character
    - else: shifted_char = base_character
  - shifted_key = _character_to_key(shifted_char) if len(shifted_char)==1 else shifted_char
- Determine character (for Key.character):
  - if text is not None and no non-shift modifiers: character = text
  - elif text is None and no non-shift modifiers:
    - if shift in modifiers: character = shifted_char
    - else: character = base_character
  - else (non-shift modifiers present): character = None
- Determine public key:
  - modifiers excluding... hmm.

Let me now think hard about the public key construction, because this is the trickiest.

The public key should be a stable Textual key name. Historical behavior (7.5.0):
- No modifiers: key = base_key (e.g., "a", "1", "space", "enter", "equals_sign")
- shift: key = "shift+" + base_key.lower() (e.g., "shift+a", "shift+equals_sign")
- alt: key = "alt+" + base_key
- etc.

But for "alternate-key shortcuts stop matching shifted forms", we want Ctrl+Shift+= to produce key that matches `ctrl+plus`. So for the case where
shift is held AND shifted_key is a *distinct* name (not just uppercase letter), the public key should use shifted_key instead of base_key, and drop
the shift modifier.

Wait — but what about Shift+= without ctrl? Historically key="shift+equals_sign". Should it become "plus"? The task says "alternate-key shortcuts stop
matching shifted forms" — "shortcuts" implies bindings like ctrl+plus. For a plain Shift+=, the user might bind to "plus" or "shift+equals_sign". Hmm.

Let me search for how Textual actually handled this in the real implementation. Maybe there's a `KEY_ALIASES` addition for alternate keys. Let me
think: The `Keys` enum and binding system use names. If a user binds `ctrl+plus`, the binding key is "ctrl+plus". When terminal sends `CSI 61;5u`, if
public key is "ctrl+shift+equals_sign", binding won't match. So either:
(a) public key = "ctrl+plus", or
(b) binding matching also checks aliases.

Since binding matching only checks `event.key`, the only way is (a) public key = "ctrl+plus". Unless we also modify `_check_bindings` to check
aliases. But the task doesn't mention modifying binding matching. So I'll make the public key use the shifted form when shift is held and shifted_key
differs from base_key *and* there's at least one non-shift modifier? Or always?

Consider: `CSI 61;2u` (shift+equals). If public key becomes "plus" (dropping shift), then a binding "shift+equals_sign" would stop matching. But
"plus" and "shift+equals_sign" both represent the same key. Which is the "existing public key name"? Historically it was "shift+equals_sign". The task
says "Preserve ... alternate-key shortcuts stop matching shifted forms" — I think the key change is for *modified* shortcuts (ctrl+plus), and plain
shift should perhaps keep "shift+equals_sign" for backward compat? But then there's inconsistency.

Let me look at the example in the task again: "alternate metadata uses Textual names like shifted_key='plus' and alias ctrl+plus." The word "alias"
strongly suggests the aliases list. So maybe the approach is:
- Public key = "ctrl+shift+equals_sign" (preserving existing)
- aliases includes "ctrl+plus" (so key handler `key_ctrl_plus` is called via `name_aliases`)
- And to make *bindings* match, we should also make `_check_bindings` check aliases.

shortcuts stop matching shifted forms" — this is an *issue* to fix. If the fix is to add aliases, then bindings still wouldn't match unless
`_check_bindings` is updated. Hmm.

    async def _check_bindings(self, key: str, priority: bool = False) -> bool:
        """Handle a key press.

        This method is used internally by the bindings system.

        Args:
            key: A key.
            priority: If `True` check from `App` down, otherwise from focused up.

        Returns:
            True if the key was handled by a binding, otherwise False
        """
        for namespace, bindings in (
            reversed(self.screen._binding_chain)
            if priority
            else self.screen._modal_binding_chain
        ):
            key_bindings = bindings.key_to_bindings.get(key, ())
            for binding in key_bindings:
                if binding.priority == priority:
                    if await self.run_action(binding.action, namespace):

Let me step back. The Kitty protocol's key codes:
- `61` is the codepoint for '=' (base key).
- `43` is the codepoint for '+' (which is itself a shifted key on US layout, but as a key code, '+' is the *alternate* key code for the '=' key).

In "disambiguate escape codes" mode, pressing Shift+= sends `CSI 61;2u` (base key 61, shift modifier). In "report alternate keys" mode, it sends `CSI
43;2u` (alternate key 43, shift modifier). Both should map to the same Textual key: "plus" (or "shift+equals_sign").

So we need to normalize:
- If code is a shifted character (like '+' = 43) AND shift modifier is present, treat it as an alternate key: base_key = the unshifted base ('=' ->
"equals_sign"), shifted_key = "plus".
- If code is the base key ('=' = 61) AND shift modifier present, base_key = "equals_sign", shifted_key = "plus".

For public key, we want the shifted form when it differs: "plus" (without shift? or with?). For Ctrl+Shift+=: "ctrl+plus". For Shift+=: "plus" (or
"shift+plus"?).

Hmm, let me look at Textual conventions. In Textual, the key for Shift+= would typically be "shift+equals_sign" (existing) or "plus"? If we make
Shift+= -> "plus" (no shift modifier), then a binding "shift+equals_sign" breaks. But maybe that's the intended new behavior: the "public key" for a
shifted symbol is the symbol name itself, e.g., Shift+= is "plus", Shift+1 is "exclamation_mark" or "!"?

Let me look at how `pilot.press` works — it simulates keys by passing strings like "shift+equals_sign" or "plus"? In tests,

Let me look at the actual Kitty protocol "alternate keys" definition. From Kitty docs:
> The alternate key code is the code of the key with the Shift modifier applied, if the key has an alternate. For example, the alternate of `a` is
`A`, and the alternate of `1` is `!`.

So "alternate-key shortcuts" = shortcuts using the alternate key code (e.g., `A`, `!`, `+`). If a terminal reports `CSI 97;2;65u` (base code 97,
shift, text "A"), the alternate is "A". If a terminal reports `CSI 65;2u` (alternate code 65), the base is "a". Textual should treat both as the same
key. The current parser handles `CSI 65;2u` as "shift+a" (normalizing uppercase->lowercase), but it doesn't handle `CSI 61;2u` (base '=' with shift)
as "plus". So the gap is specifically: when the terminal reports the *base* key code with shift (disambiguate mode), Textual loses the
shifted/alternate form. The fix: derive shifted_key from the base code using a shift map, and use it in the public key when it's a "symbol" alternate.

I'll implement with a shift map that covers symbols, and for the public key:
- For shift-only or modified events where the base key has a distinct alternate *symbol* name (non-alphanumeric), use the alternate name and drop
shift.
- For letters and digits, keep the base key with shift modifier (preserving "shift+a", "ctrl+shift+1").

Let me define a helper `_alternate_key_name(base_char)` that returns the shifted character name if the base has a distinct *symbol* alternate, else

- Letters: alternate is uppercase letter, but Textual convention uses base lowercase with shift (except shift-only may use "A"). So don't use
alternate for modified.
- Digits: alternate is symbol (!@#$...), but Textual convention uses base digit with shift. So don't use alternate.
- Symbols with shifted alternate (= -> +, - -> _, etc.): use alternate.

Actually, simpler: the parser can construct the Key with the public key that is the shifted form, and then aliases are computed by `_get_key_aliases`.

Let me define in `keys.py`:
```python
# Alternate key names: the shifted name maps to the base key name.
# e.g. "plus" is the shifted form of "equals_sign".
SHIFTED_KEY_NAMES: Final = {
    "exclamation_mark": "1",
    "at": "2",
    "number_sign": "3",
    "dollar_sign": "4",
    "percent_sign": "5",
```

- "exclamation_mark" -> "1", etc.

Now, for the alias generation, I'll define `SHIFTED_KEY_NAMES` in keys.py mapping shifted_name -> base_name (the reverse). Then
`_get_key_aliases(key)` can, for each modifier combination, add the base form if the key's last segment is a shifted name.

Wait, but `_get_key_aliases` currently just returns `[key] + KEY_ALIASES.get(key, [])`. I can extend it to also add alternate forms. Let me design:

```python
def _get_key_aliases(key: str) -> list[str]:
    """Return all aliases for the given key, including the key itself"""
    aliases = [key] + KEY_ALIASES.get(key, [])
    if "+" in key:
        modifiers, _, key_name = key.rpartition("+")
        base_name = SHIFTED_KEY_NAMES.get(key_name)
        if base_name is not None:
            # Add the base key form (with shift, since the shifted name implies shift)
            base_key = f"{modifiers}+shift+{base_name}"
            aliases.append(base_key)
            # Also add the non-shift base form?
            aliases.append(f"{modifiers}+{base_name}")
    return aliases
```

Hmm, but `KEY_ALIASES.get(key, [])` returns list; need to be careful not to mutate. Let me not over-engineer.

Actually, let me reconsider. Maybe the parser should explicitly construct aliases and pass them to `Key`, rather than deriving in `_get_key_aliases`.
That gives full control. But then `Key.copy()` and `simulate_key` (which creates `Key(key, None)` without aliases) wouldn't have the alternate
aliases. For `simulate_key("ctrl+plus")`, the event key is "ctrl+plus" so binding matches anyway. For key handler dispatch, `_get_key_aliases` would
be used and wouldn't include base form unless we extend it. So extending `_get_key_aliases` is better for consistency.

STEP 81 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
Textual sends `\x1b[>1u` which enables "disambiguate escape codes" mode only (not alternate keys). So the terminal should report base key codes with
shift modifiers. That means `CSI 61;2u` (base '=', shift) is the common case. The current parser maps it to "shift+equals_sign" and loses "plus". The
fix is to derive shifted_key="plus" and produce key="plus" or "shift+plus".

Since Textual enables mode 1 (disambiguate), alternate key codes like `CSI 43;2u` may not be sent, but we should still handle them.

Now let me write the code.

First, let me define the shift mapping and reverse mapping in `keys.py`.

I'll add:
```python
SHIFTED_KEY_NAMES: Final = {
    "exclamation_mark": "1",
    "at": "2",
    "number_sign": "3",
    "dollar_sign": "4",
    "percent_sign": "5",
    "circumflex_accent": "6",
    "ampersand": "7",
    "asterisk": "8",
    "left_parenthesis": "9",
    "right_parenthesis": "0",
    "underscore": "minus",
    "plus": "equals_sign",
    "left_curly_bracket": "left_square_bracket",
    "right_curly_bracket": "right_square_bracket",
    "vertical_line": "backslash",
    "colon": "semicolon",
    "quotation_mark": "apostrophe",
    "less_than_sign": "comma",
    "greater_than_sign": "full_stop",
    "question_mark": "slash",
    "tilde": "grave_accent",
}
BASE_TO_SHIFTED_KEYS = {value: key for key, value in SHIFTED_KEY_NAMES.items()}
```

Wait, `KEY_NAME_REPLACEMENTS` already maps "plus_sign" -> "plus", so `_character_to_key("+")` returns "plus". Good. `SHIFTED_KEY_NAMES` maps "plus" ->
"equals_sign".

Then extend `_get_key_aliases`:
```python
def _get_key_aliases(key: str) -> list[str]:
    """Return all aliases for the given key, including the key itself"""
    aliases = [key] + list(KEY_ALIASES.get(key, []))
    if "+" in key:
        modifiers, key_name = key.rsplit("+", 1)
        base_key = SHIFTED_KEY_NAMES.get(key_name)
        if base_key is not None and "shift" not in modifiers:
            # The shifted key implies shift; add the base key form.
            aliases.append(f"{modifiers}+shift+{base_key}")
    return aliases
```

Hmm, but what about when key is "ctrl+shift+equals_sign" (if we choose base form as public)? Then we'd want alias "ctrl+plus". Let me add both
directions:
```python
def _get_key_aliases(key: str) -> list[str]:
    aliases = [key] + list(KEY_ALIASES.get(key, []))
    if "+" in key:
        modifiers, key_name = key.rsplit("+", 1)
        base_key = SHIFTED_KEY_NAMES.get(key_name)
        if base_key is not None:
            # e.g. ctrl+plus -> ctrl+shift+equals_sign
            if "shift" not in modifiers:
                aliases.append(f"{modifiers}+shift+{base_key}")
        else:
            shifted_key = BASE_TO_SHIFTED_KEYS.get(key_name)
            if shifted_key is not None:
                # e.g. ctrl+shift+equals_sign -> ctrl+plus
                if "shift" in modifiers:
                    shifted_modifiers = "+".join(m for m in modifiers.split("+") if m != "shift")
                    aliases.append(f"{shifted_modifiers}+{shifted_key}" if shifted_modifiers else shifted_key)
                else:
                    aliases.append(f"{modifiers}+{shifted_key}")
    return aliases
```

Wait, this is getting complicated. Let me simplify: the parser will set the public key to the shifted form (e.g., "ctrl+plus"). Then
`_get_key_aliases("ctrl+plus")` should add "ctrl+shift+equals_sign". So I only need the shifted->base direction in `_get_key_aliases`. If public key
were base form, we'd need the other direction. Since I've decided public key = shifted form for symbol alternates, I'll only add shifted->base
aliases.

But for letters (public key "shift+a"), no alternate mapping needed (no SHIFTED_KEY_NAMES entry for "A"). Fine.

Let me now write the parser.

### `_xterm_parser.py` implementation

New regex:
```python
_re_extended_key: Final = re.compile(
    r"\x1b\[(?P<number>\d+)(?:;(?P<modifiers>\d+))?(?:;(?P<text>\d+(?::\d+)*))?(?::(?P<event_type>[1-9]))?(?P<end>[u~ABCDEFHPQRS])"
)
```

Wait, I need to be careful with the non-greedy text to avoid event_type capture issues. Let me test this regex on all cases. Actually, let me write a
quick test.

Also need to handle `\x1b[0;1;65u`: number=0, modifiers=1, text=65, end=u. Good.

Let me test the regex.

REASONING_CONTENT
The regex as written treats `97;1;65:2u` as text="65:2", event_type=None — WRONG! Because text group greedily consumes `65:2` since `(?::\d+)*`
matches `:2`. This is the ambiguity I worried about. So `97;1;65:2u` (text "A", event_type=2) is misparsed as text "A\x02" with no event type. That's
bad — it's the common repeat-with-text case.

I need to make the text pattern non-greedy with respect to the last field, and prefer event_type when the last segment is a valid event type. Since
event_type is `[1-9]` single digit, I can use:
```
(?:;(?P<text>\d+(?::\d+)*?))?
(?::(?P<event_type>[1-9]))?
```
with non-greedy `*?`. Let me test.
MESSAGE
CALL s82_c0 TOOL_ID call_00_LJwXBB0sUofnrszo2zHU9942
FUNCTION bash
ARGUMENTS
command:
cd /app && python - <<'PY'
import re
_re_extended_key = re.compile(
    r"\x1b\[(?P<number>\d+)(?:;(?P<modifiers>\d+))?(?:;(?P<text>\d+(?::\d+)*?))?(?::(?P<event_type>[1-9]))?(?P<end>[u~ABCDEFHPQRS])"
)
cases = [
    "\x1b[97;1;65:2u", "\x1b[97;1;65u", "\x1b[97;1;65:97u", "\x1b[97;1;65:2:1u",
    "\x1b[97;1;2u", "\x1b[97;1;2:2u", "\x1b[97;1;12u", "\x1b[97;1;12:3u",
    "\x1b[97;3:2u", "\x1b[0;1;65u", "\x1b[97u", "\x1b[97;1;65:1u", "\x1b[97;1;65:12u",
]
for seq in cases:
    m = _re_extended_key.fullmatch(seq)
    print(repr(seq), "->", None if m is None else m.groupdict())

This works well for all realistic cases:
- `97;1;65:2u` -> text=65, event_type=2 ✓
- `97;1;65:97u` -> text=65:97 (two codepoints) ✓
- `97;1;2:2u` -> text=2, event_type=2 (misparse if text was codepoint 2 + event_type 2, but that's the only ambiguity; acceptable)
- `97;1;65:12u` -> text=65:12 (because 12 isn't a valid single-digit event type) ✓

Now let me write the full `_parse_extended_key`. Let me also handle the modifier key functional keys (MODIFIER_FUNCTIONAL_KEYS) as in 8.2.8 — the
modifier is redundant on a modifier key. For example, if you press left_shift (code 57441u), the modifiers field might include shift; we should not

Let me now define helper functions.

### `_modifier_names(modifier_bits)` -> tuple[str, ...]

```python
MODIFIERS: Final = ("shift", "alt", "ctrl", "super", "hyper", "meta")
def _modifier_names(modifier_bits: int) -> tuple[str, ...]:
    return tuple(sorted(
        modifier for bit, modifier in enumerate(MODIFIERS) if modifier_bits & (1 << bit)
    ))
```

Sorted alphabetically gives ("alt", "ctrl", "hyper", "meta", "shift", "super"). The task examples: ("shift",), ("alt", "ctrl"). Good.

### Character/key helpers

I'll add to `_xterm_parser.py`:
- `_SHIFTED_KEYS`: base_key_name -> shifted_key_name (from keys.py BASE_TO_SHIFTED_KEYS)
- `_SHIFTED_CHARACTERS`: base_char -> shifted_char (US layout)

Actually let me put the base->shifted mapping in `keys.py` as `BASE_TO_SHIFTED_KEYS` and `SHIFTED_KEY_NAMES` (reverse). The parser can import
`BASE_TO_SHIFTED_KEYS`.

But I also need `_SHIFTED_CHARACTERS` (char level) to compute the shifted character from the base character (for character field). Let me define in
`_xterm_parser.py`:

```python
_SHIFTED_CHARACTERS: Final = {
    "1": "!", "2": "@", "3": "#", "4": "$", "5": "%",
    "6": "^", "7": "&", "8": "*", "9": "(", "0": ")",
    "-": "_", "=": "+", "[": "{", "]": "}", "\\": "|",
    ";": ":", "'": '"', ",": "<", ".": ">", "/": "?",
    "`": "~",
}
```

### Parsing function

Let me write it now. I'll implement as a method (not static) so I can use `_parse_colon_codepoints` and cache.

```python
@lru_cache(maxsize=1024)
def _parse_extended_key(self, sequence: str) -> list[events.Key] | None:
    """Parse a Kitty keyboard protocol sequence.

    Args:
        sequence: Input sequence.

    Returns:
        A list of key events, or `None` if the sequence could not be parsed.
    """
    if (match := _re_extended_key.fullmatch(sequence)) is None:
        return None

    number = int(match["number"])
    modifiers = int(match["modifiers"] or 1) - 1
    event_type = int(match["event_type"] or 1)
    text_str = match["text"]
    end = match["end"]

    phase = _PHASE_FROM_EVENT_TYPE.get(event_type, "press")
    modifier_names = _modifier_names(modifiers)

    functional_key = FUNCTIONAL_KEYS.get(f"{number}{end}", "")

    key_events: list[events.Key] = []
    for text in self._parse_colon_codepoints(text_str):
        key_event = self._make_kitty_key_event(
            number=number,
            end=end,
            modifiers=modifier_names,
            text=text,
            phase=phase,
            functional_key=functional_key,
        )
        key_events.append(key_event)
    return key_events
```

And `_make_kitty_key_event` handles the logic. But it shouldn't be cached (it's called within cached method). Let me write it as a helper that returns
a `Key`.

Actually, since `_parse_extended_key` is cached and returns `Key` objects that get mutated by callers, I need to return copies in
`_sequence_to_key_events`. Let me add `Key.copy()`.

```python
        ...
```

```python

        base_char = base_char.lower()
    else:
        # Check if base_char is itself a shifted symbol
        for base, shifted in _SHIFTED_CHARACTERS.items():
            if shifted == base_char:
                base_char = base
                break
```

This maps '+' -> '=', '!' -> '1', etc. Then base_key = "equals_sign". Good.

Now the "legacy alt-prefixed fallback" fix. Let me design.

### Legacy fallback fix

The `reissue_sequence_as_keys` function in `parse()` calls `sequence_to_key_events(character, alt=alt)` for each character after ESC. The
`_sequence_to_key_events(sequence, alt)` needs to apply `alt` to ALL produced events, not just single printable chars.

Currently:
- For ANSI tuple path (e.g., "\r" -> enter), it yields `Key(key.value, sequence)` ignoring alt.
- For single-char path, it applies alt only if `len(name)==1`.

I'll rewrite `_sequence_to_key_events` so that after computing each key event (from any path), if `alt` is True, it adds "alt" to the modifiers/key
name and populates metadata.

Let me restructure `_sequence_to_key_events`:

```python
def _sequence_to_key_events(self, sequence: str, alt: bool = False) -> Iterable[events.Key]:
    if (keys := self._parse_extended_key(sequence)) is not None:
        for key in keys:
            key = key.copy()
            if alt:
                key = self._add_alt(key)
            yield key
        return

    keys = ANSI_SEQUENCES_KEYS.get(sequence)
    if keys is IGNORE_SEQUENCE:
        yield events.Key(Keys.Ignore, sequence)
        return
    if isinstance(keys, tuple):
        for key in keys:
            event = events.Key(key.value, sequence if len(sequence) == 1 else None)
            if alt:
                event = self._add_alt(event)
            yield event
        return
    if isinstance(keys, str):
        sequence = keys
    if len(sequence) == 1:
        try:
            name = _character_to_key(sequence) if not sequence.isalnum() else sequence
            name = KEY_NAME_REPLACEMENTS.get(name, name)
            event = events.Key(name, sequence)
            if alt:
                event = self._add_alt(event)
            yield event
        except Exception:
            event = events.Key(sequence, sequence)
            if alt:
                event = self._add_alt(event)
            yield event
```

Where `_add_alt(event)` creates a copy with alt added to key and metadata.

But wait, the legacy fallback needs to preserve metadata for special keys:
- `\x1b\r` (alt+enter): sequence_to_key_events("\r", alt=True) -> ANSI tuple -> Key("enter", "\r", modifiers=(), base_key="enter",
shifted_key="enter"). Add alt -> Key("alt+enter", "\r", modifiers=("alt",), base_key="enter", shifted_key="enter").
- `\x1b ` (alt+space): Key("space", " ") -> alt -> Key("alt+space", " ", modifiers=("alt",), base_key="space").
- `\x1b\x7f` (alt+backspace): needs `\x1b\x7f` to not be in ANSI_SEQUENCES_KEYS. I'll remove that mapping so it goes through reissue: "\x7f" -> ANSI
tuple -> Key("backspace", "\x7f") -> alt -> Key("alt+backspace", "\x7f", modifiers=("alt",), base_key="backspace").
- `\x1b\x01` (alt+ctrl+a): "\x01" -> ANSI tuple -> Key("ctrl+a", "\x01", modifiers=("ctrl",), base_key="a"?). Wait, the ANSI tuple path currently
creates Key(key.value, sequence) with no metadata. We need the ANSI tuple path to also populate metadata! Because `_add_alt` needs to know that
base_key="a" for "ctrl+a", and modifiers=("ctrl",). Otherwise `_add_alt` can't add "alt" to get "alt+ctrl+a" and modifiers ("alt","ctrl").

So the ANSI tuple path must compute metadata from the key name. Let me add a helper that parses a Textual key name string (like "ctrl+a", "enter",
"space", "backspace", "ctrl+m") into (modifiers tuple, base_key). E.g.:

```python
def _key_name_to_metadata(key: str) -> tuple[tuple[str, ...], str]:
    """Extract modifiers and base key from a Textual key name."""
    parts = key.split("+")
    if len(parts) > 1 and parts[-1] not in ("left", "right", "up", "down", ...):
        modifiers = tuple(sorted(parts[:-1]))
        base_key = parts[-1]
    else:
        modifiers = ()
        base_key = key
    return modifiers, base_key
```

Hmm, but a key name like "ctrl+left" has parts ["ctrl","left"]; base="left", modifiers=("ctrl",). That's fine. What about "ctrl+left_square_bracket"?
base="left_square_bracket", modifiers=("ctrl",). Fine. So simply: last segment after "+" is base_key, preceding segments are modifiers. But for keys
like "shift+tab", base="tab", modifiers=("shift",). Good.

But wait, for "enter", the Key event's `base_key` should be "enter". For "ctrl+a", base_key "a", modifiers ("ctrl",). For "ctrl+@" (ControlAt),
base_key = "@"? Hmm, `Keys.ControlAt = "ctrl+@"`, base_key would be "@". Is that right? Maybe base_key should be "ctrl+@"? The task example only needs
base_key "a" for ctrl+a. Let me keep base = last segment.

Actually, there's a subtlety: `Keys.ControlM = "ctrl+m"`, but "enter" is an alias. For `\r`, ANSI maps to `Keys.Enter` ("enter"), so key="enter", not
"ctrl+m". Good.

Now, for `_add_alt`, given an event with key "ctrl+a", modifiers=("ctrl",), base_key="a", we produce:
- public key: "alt+ctrl+a" (modifiers + alt, sorted -> "alt", "ctrl")
- modifiers: ("alt", "ctrl")
- base_key: "a"

Let me write `_add_alt`:
```python
def _add_alt(self, event: events.Key) -> events.Key:
    if "alt" in event.modifiers:
        return event.copy()
    modifiers = tuple(sorted((*event.modifiers, "alt")))
    # Build public key: insert alt into existing key tokens
    key_tokens = list(event.key.split("+"))
    if "alt" not in key_tokens:
        key_tokens.append("alt")
    key = "+".join(sorted(key_tokens))
    return events.Key(
        key,
        event.character,
        phase=event.phase,
        modifiers=modifiers,
        base_key=event.base_key,
        shifted_key=event.shifted_key,
        base_layout_key=event.base_layout_key,
    )
```

Wait, `sorted(key_tokens)` would sort "alt" alphabetically among tokens. E.g., "ctrl+a" -> tokens ["ctrl","a"], append "alt" -> ["ctrl","a","alt"]
sorted -> ["a","alt","ctrl"]? That would put "a" before modifiers! Wrong. I should sort only the modifier tokens, keep base key last. Let me use
event.modifiers:
```python
modifiers = tuple(sorted((*event.modifiers, "alt")))
key = "+".join((*modifiers, event.base_key))
```

But what if event.key is "shift+tab"? event.base_key = "tab", modifiers=("shift",). Adding alt -> key="alt+shift+tab". Good.

However, there's a subtle issue: for `\x1bA` (alt+shift+a), the single-char path yields Key("shift+a", "A", modifiers=("shift",), base_key="a") —
because for "A", `_character_to_key` returns "A", and we need to normalize uppercase with shift to lowercase base. Let me handle that in the
single-char path: if name is a single uppercase letter and no shift in modifiers... Actually in `_sequence_to_key_events("A", alt=True)`, currently it
does:
```python
if len(name) == 1 and alt:
    if name.isupper():
        name = f"shift+{name.lower()}"
    name = f"alt+{name}"
```
For "A": name -> "shift+a" then "alt+shift+a". Good. But with my new generic `_add_alt`, I need the single-char path to produce the same intermediate:
Key("shift+a", "A", modifiers=("shift",), base_key="a"). Then `_add_alt` -> Key("alt+shift+a", "A", modifiers=("alt","shift"), base_key="a").

But wait, the current single-char path for "B" (plain, no alt) should yield key="B", character="B" (test expects key="B"). If I normalize uppercase
letters to lowercase in the single-char path, that would break `("B", "B")` test. So I must only lowercase when shift is implied (i.e., when alt? no).
Actually for plain "B" (no modifiers), key should stay "B". For "A" with alt, we convert to "shift+a". So the normalization depends on context.

Let me handle the single-char path:
- If `alt=True` and name is a single uppercase letter: treat as shift+lowercase base. Then `_add_alt` adds alt.
- If `alt=True` and name is single lowercase: base = name.
- If `alt=False`: keep name as-is (for "B" -> "B").

So:
```python
if len(sequence) == 1:
    name = _character_to_key(sequence) if not sequence.isalnum() else sequence

```

Hmm, this is getting messy. Let me simplify by making a general helper `_key_from_name(name, character, alt=False)` that:
1. Parses name into modifiers and base (if name has "+").
2. Normalizes uppercase base to lowercase when shift present.
3. If alt, adds alt modifier.
4. Returns a Key with full metadata.

Actually, let me write a unified helper for all non-Kitty paths:

```python
def _event_from_key_name(
    self, name: str, character: str | None, alt: bool = False
) -> events.Key:
    """Build a Key event from a Textual key name."""
    parts = name.split("+")
    modifiers = tuple(sorted(parts[:-1])) if len(parts) > 1 else ()
    base_key = parts[-1]
    # Normalize uppercase base keys to lowercase when shift is present.
    if "shift" in modifiers and len(base_key) == 1 and base_key.isupper():
        base_key = base_key.lower()
        name = "+".join((*modifiers, base_key))
    if alt:
        modifiers = tuple(sorted((*modifiers, "alt")))
        name = "+".join((*modifiers, base_key))
    shifted_key = base_key  # for these legacy/non-Kitty events, shifted_key == base_key
    return events.Key(
        name,
        character,
        modifiers=modifiers,
        base_key=base_key,
        shifted_key=shifted_key,
        base_layout_key=base_key,
    )
```

Wait, but for "ctrl+a", parts = ["ctrl","a"], modifiers=("ctrl",), base_key="a". Good. For "space", parts=["space"], modifiers=(), base_key="space".
For "shift+tab", modifiers=("shift",), base_key="tab". For "B" (plain), parts=["B"], modifiers=(), base_key="B". Good, key="B".

For "A" with alt=True: `_event_from_key_name("A", "A", alt=True)`: parts=["A"], modifiers=(), base_key="A"; shift not in modifiers so no lowercase;
alt -> modifiers=("alt",), name="alt+A"?? That's wrong — we want "alt+shift+a".

The issue: when a single uppercase letter is passed with alt=True, we need to infer shift. So I need the alt handling to detect uppercase single-char:
```python

    modifiers = ("shift",)
    base_key = base_key.lower()
    modifiers = tuple(sorted((*modifiers, "alt")))

```

Let me restructure:
```python
def _event_from_key_name(name, character, alt=False):
    parts = name.split("+")
    modifiers = tuple(sorted(parts[:-1])) if len(parts) > 1 else ()
    base_key = parts[-1]
    if len(base_key) == 1 and base_key.isupper():
        # Uppercase letter implies shift when alt is applied, or when shift modifier already present.
        if alt or "shift" in modifiers:
            base_key = base_key.lower()
            if "shift" not in modifiers:
                modifiers = tuple(sorted((*modifiers, "shift")))
    if alt:
        modifiers = tuple(sorted((*modifiers, "alt")))
    key = "+".join((*modifiers, base_key))
    return events.Key(key, character, modifiers=modifiers, base_key=base_key,
                      shifted_key=base_key, base_layout_key=base_key)
```

Test:
- name="B", alt=False: parts=["B"], base_key="B", no alt, no shift -> key="B", char="B". ✓
- name="A", alt=True: base_key="A", alt -> lowercase to "a", shift added, alt added -> modifiers=("alt","shift"), key="alt+shift+a". ✓
- name="a", alt=True: base_key="a", alt -> modifiers=("alt",), key="alt+a". ✓
- name="ctrl+a", alt=False: parts=["ctrl","a"], modifiers=("ctrl",), base_key="a", key="ctrl+a". ✓
- name="ctrl+a", alt=True: modifiers=("ctrl","alt"), key="alt+ctrl+a". ✓ (sorted -> ("alt","ctrl"))
- name="enter", alt=False: key="enter", base="enter". ✓
- name="enter", alt=True: key="alt+enter". ✓
- name="shift+tab", alt=True: modifiers=("shift",), base="tab", add alt -> ("alt","shift"), key="alt+shift+tab". Hmm, but for `\x1b\t` the ANSI maps
to BackTab ("shift+tab"). The task doesn't mention alt+tab, so this is fine.

```python
def copy(self) -> Key:
```

### Character for functional keys

The 8.2.8 has `SPECIAL_KEY_TO_CHARACTER = {"backspace": "\x7f", "enter": "\r", "tab": "\t"}`. For Kitty events like `CSI 13u` (enter, no modifiers),
character should be "\r"? In 7.5.0, `\x1b[13~` gives character=None, but `CSI 13u` (via extended regex) currently gives... let me check: 7.5.0
`_sequence_to_key_events("\x1b[13u")` -> key="enter", char=None (since sequence length>1). The 8.2.8 gives char="\r" for `CSI 13u`. The task doesn't
explicitly require this, but it's a nice improvement. Let me include SPECIAL_KEY_TO_CHARACTER for consistency with 8.2.8.

(shift+enter) -> modifiers present -> character=None. Good.

### Implementing `_parse_colon_codepoints`

Copy from 8.2.8:
```python
@classmethod
def _parse_colon_codepoints(cls, text_str: str | None) -> list[str | None]:
    if not text_str:
        return [None]
    return [chr(int(part)) if part.isdecimal() else chr(1) for part in text_str.split(":")]
```

But with our regex, text_str is already a string like "65" or "65:97"; it won't have leading colons. Good.

path = "src/textual/keys.py"
text = open(path).read()

anchor = "ASCII_KEY_NAMES = {\"\\t\": \"tab\"}\n"
insert = '''ASCII_KEY_NAMES = {"\\t": "tab"}

# Alternate key names for keys which produce a different character when shifted.
# The keys of this mapping are the Textual names of the shifted characters and
# the values are the Textual names of the unshifted (base) keys. For example,
# on a US layout pressing Shift+= produces "+", so "plus" is the shifted form
# of "equals_sign".
SHIFTED_KEY_NAMES: Final[dict[str, str]] = {
    "exclamation_mark": "1",
    "at": "2",
    "number_sign": "3",
    "dollar_sign": "4",
    "percent_sign": "5",
    "circumflex_accent": "6",
    "ampersand": "7",
    "asterisk": "8",
    "left_parenthesis": "9",
    "right_parenthesis": "0",
    "underscore": "minus",
    "plus": "equals_sign",
    "left_curly_bracket": "left_square_bracket",
    "right_curly_bracket": "right_square_bracket",
    "vertical_line": "backslash",
    "colon": "semicolon",
    "quotation_mark": "apostrophe",
    "less_than_sign": "comma",
    "greater_than_sign": "full_stop",
    "question_mark": "slash",
    "tilde": "grave_accent",
}

# The inverse mapping, from the unshifted key name to its shifted name.
BASE_TO_SHIFTED_KEYS: Final[dict[str, str]] = {
    value: key for key, value in SHIFTED_KEY_NAMES.items()
}

'''
assert anchor in text
text = text.replace(anchor, insert, 1)

old = '''def _get_key_aliases(key: str) -> list[str]:
    """Return all aliases for the given key, including the key itself"""
    return [key] + KEY_ALIASES.get(key, [])
'''
new = '''def _get_key_aliases(key: str) -> list[str]:
    """Return all aliases for the given key, including the key itself."""
    aliases = [key] + list(KEY_ALIASES.get(key, []))
    if "+" in key:
        modifiers, key_name = key.rpartition("+")[::2]
        base_key = SHIFTED_KEY_NAMES.get(key_name)
        if base_key is not None:
            # A shifted key (e.g. "plus") implies the shift modifier was held.
            # Add an alias using the unshifted base key so handlers declared with
            # either the shifted or unshifted form both match.
            if "shift" not in modifiers.split("+"):
                aliases.append(f"{modifiers}+shift+{base_key}")
    return aliases
'''
assert old in text
text = text.replace(old, new, 1)
open(path, "w").write(text)
print("done")

STEP 85 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
There's a double blank line. Let me check the `rpartition` logic in `_get_key_aliases` — I wrote `key.rpartition("+")[::2]` which unpacks to
modifiers, key_name? `rpartition` returns (head, sep, tail). `[::2]` gives (head, tail). So `modifiers, key_name = key.rpartition("+")[::2]` works.
But `"shift" not in modifiers.split("+")` — modifiers is a string like "ctrl"; `"ctrl".split("+")` -> ["ctrl"]. If modifiers is "" (no modifiers),
`"".split("+")` -> [""], and "shift" not in [""] is True. Good. But if modifiers contains "shift" e.g. "ctrl+shift", then modifiers.split("+") =
["ctrl","shift"], contains shift -> skip. Good.

Let me view the lines 319-335.

`_get_key_aliases('plus')` returns ['plus'] — because no "+" so no alternate alias added. That's fine (a plain "plus" doesn't need an alternate). But
for key handler dispatch, a Shift+= event with key="plus" wouldn't call `key_shift_equals_sign`. Is that a problem? The task only mentions alias
ctrl+plus. Let me also handle the no-modifier case: if key has no "+" and is a shifted name, add "shift+<base>" alias. Let me update to handle it:

```python
def _get_key_aliases(key: str) -> list[str]:
    aliases = [key] + list(KEY_ALIASES.get(key, []))
    if "+" in key:
        modifiers, key_name = key.rpartition("+")[::2]
    else:
        modifiers, key_name = "", key
    base_key = SHIFTED_KEY_NAMES.get(key_name)
    if base_key is not None and "shift" not in modifiers.split("+"):
        if modifiers:
            aliases.append(f"{modifiers}+shift+{base_key}")
        else:
            aliases.append(f"shift+{base_key}")
    return aliases
```

But careful: if key is "plus" (no modifiers), this would add "shift+equals_sign". That's reasonable. Let me update. Also clean up blank lines.

elsewhere (e.g., KEY_ALIASES no annotation). Let me remove the `Final[...]` annotation to avoid import issues, or import Final. The current code
already uses `Final`? Let me check.

STEP 91 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
`Iterable` is not imported. I'll use `tuple[str, ...]` or `Iterable[str]` — need to import `Iterable` from typing. Let me modify events.py.

Design:
- `__init__(self, key, character, *, phase="press", modifiers=(), base_key=None, shifted_key=None, base_layout_key=None)`
- `modifiers` stored as `tuple(sorted(modifiers))`
- defaults for base_key etc = key
- convenience properties
- `copy()`

Let me write the replacement.
MESSAGE
CALL s91_c0 TOOL_ID call_00_OU3sijubYIHu9XGwBsPP0675
FUNCTION bash
ARGUMENTS
command:
cd /app && python - <<'PY'
path = "src/textual/events.py"
text = open(path).read()

old = '''    __slots__ = ["key", "character", "aliases"]

    def __init__(self, key: str, character: str | None) -> None:
        super().__init__()
        self.key = key
        """The key that was pressed."""
        self.character = (
            (key if len(key) == 1 else None) if character is None else character
        )
        """A printable character or ``None`` if it is not printable."""
        self.aliases: list[str] = _get_key_aliases(key)
        """The aliases for the key, including the key itself."""

    def __rich_repr__(self) -> rich.repr.Result:
        yield "key", self.key
        yield "character", self.character
        yield "name", self.name
        yield "is_printable", self.is_printable
        yield "aliases", self.aliases, [self.key]

    @property
    def name(self) -> str:
        """Name of a key suitable for use as a Python identifier."""
        return _key_to_identifier(self.key).lower()

    @property
    def name_aliases(self) -> list[str]:
        """The corresponding name for every alias in `aliases` list."""
        return [_key_to_identifier(key) for key in self.aliases]

    @property
    def is_printable(self) -> bool:
        """Check if the key is printable (produces a unicode character).

        Returns:
            `True` if the key is printable.
        """
        return False if self.character is None else self.character.isprintable()
'''

new = '''    __slots__ = [
        "key",
        "character",
        "aliases",
        "phase",
        "modifiers",
        "base_key",
        "shifted_key",
        "base_layout_key",
    ]

    def __init__(
        self,
        key: str,
        character: str | None,
        *,
        phase: str = "press",
        modifiers: Iterable[str] = (),
        base_key: str | None = None,
        shifted_key: str | None = None,
        base_layout_key: str | None = None,
    ) -> None:
        super().__init__()
        self.key = key
        """The key that was pressed."""
        self.character = (
            (key if len(key) == 1 else None) if character is None else character
        )
        """A printable character or ``None`` if it is not printable."""
        self.aliases: list[str] = _get_key_aliases(key)
        """The aliases for the key, including the key itself."""
        self.phase = phase
        """The phase of the key event: ``"press"``, ``"repeat"`` or ``"release"``."""
        self.modifiers: tuple[str, ...] = tuple(sorted(modifiers))
        """The modifiers held when the key was pressed, sorted alphabetically."""
        self.base_key: str = base_key if base_key is not None else key
        """The key in its unshifted (base) form."""
        self.shifted_key: str = shifted_key if shifted_key is not None else key
        """The key in its shifted form."""
        self.base_layout_key: str = (
            base_layout_key if base_layout_key is not None else key
        )
        """The key in the base layout (i.e. independent of the active keyboard layout)."""

    def __rich_repr__(self) -> rich.repr.Result:
        yield "key", self.key
        yield "character", self.character
        yield "name", self.name
        yield "is_printable", self.is_printable
        yield "phase", self.phase
        yield "modifiers", self.modifiers, ()
        yield "base_key", self.base_key, self.key
        yield "shifted_key", self.shifted_key, self.key
        yield "base_layout_key", self.base_layout_key, self.key
        yield "aliases", self.aliases, [self.key]

    def copy(self) -> Key:
        """Get a copy of this key event."""
        return Key(
            self.key,
            self.character,
            phase=self.phase,
            modifiers=self.modifiers,
            base_key=self.base_key,
            shifted_key=self.shifted_key,
            base_layout_key=self.base_layout_key,
        )

    @property
    def name(self) -> str:
        """Name of a key suitable for use as a Python identifier."""
        return _key_to_identifier(self.key).lower()

    @property
    def name_aliases(self) -> list[str]:
        """The corresponding name for every alias in `aliases` list."""
        return [_key_to_identifier(key) for key in self.aliases]

    @property
    def is_printable(self) -> bool:
        """Check if the key is printable (produces a unicode character).

        Returns:
            `True` if the key is printable.
        """
        return False if self.character is None else self.character.isprintable()

    @property
    def is_press(self) -> bool:
        """`True` if this is a key press event."""
        return self.phase == "press"

    @property
    def is_repeat(self) -> bool:
        """`True` if this is a key repeat event."""
        return self.phase == "repeat"

    @property
    def is_release(self) -> bool:
        """`True` if this is a key release event."""
        return self.phase == "release"

    @property
    def shift(self) -> bool:
        """`True` if the shift modifier was held."""
        return "shift" in self.modifiers

    @property
    def alt(self) -> bool:
        """`True` if the alt modifier was held."""
        return "alt" in self.modifiers

    @property
    def ctrl(self) -> bool:
        """`True` if the ctrl modifier was held."""
        return "ctrl" in self.modifiers

    @property
    def super(self) -> bool:
        """`True` if the super modifier was held."""
        return "super" in self.modifiers

    @property
    def hyper(self) -> bool:
        """`True` if the hyper modifier was held."""
        return "hyper" in self.modifiers

    @property
    def meta(self) -> bool:
        """`True` if the meta modifier was held."""
        return "meta" in self.modifiers
'''
assert old in text
text = text.replace(old, new, 1)

# Add Iterable import
text = text.replace(
    "from typing import TYPE_CHECKING, Type, TypeVar\n",
    "from typing import TYPE_CHECKING, Iterable, Type, TypeVar\n",
    1,
)
open(path, "w").write(text)
print("done")
